Svara-Mātṛkā-Bījākṣara Saṅgamaḥ — Module III: A Hundred Kṛtis and Fifteen Enduring Questions
Module III of a three-part study · Module I — Rasautpatti, Kāvyotpatti, Nāṭyotpatti · Module II — Fifty Kṛtis, A Case-Study Index — this module extends both into Sanskrit phonetics and the 52 bījākṣara
Field index · one hundred compositions, five articulation-points, ten enduring questions

स्वर-मातृका-बीजाक्षर सङ्गमःSvara-Mātṛkā-Bījākṣara Saṅgamaḥ

Where Module I built the rasa-mechanics and Module II tested it against fifty kṛtis, this third module turns to a different substrate entirely: the seven svara and their sixteen varieties read against the fifty-two bījākṣara of the mātṛkā alphabet, cross-referenced through the five points of consonantal articulation (kaṇṭhya, tālavya, mūrdhanya, dantya, oṣṭhya) that Sanskrit phonetics and Śrī Vidyā doctrine both already use to organize sound. A hundred kṛtis are indexed against this framework, followed by fifteen of the tradition's oldest and least resolved questions — about embodiment, variation, and unearned suffering — read through the vocabulary this framework provides.

100Kṛtis indexed
52Bījākṣara mapped
5Articulation-point groups
15Research questions
Read first

What Part Nine of this module is, and is not. The ten questions in Part IX concern embodiment, human variation, and suffering — including why some people are born with disabilities and carry hardship across a lifetime. The answers given there are a careful, sourced account of what classical Vedāntic, Sāṃkhya, and Tantric philosophy has historically said about these questions — karma, prārabdha, guṇa-vaicitrya, avidyā — presented as doctrine, with its own internal logic and its own centuries of internal debate. They are not, and are not offered as, a scientific or medical account of why any individual has a disability or an illness. The actual causes of congenital and acquired disabilities are genetic, developmental, prenatal, and environmental, and are the province of medicine and genetics, not of śāstra; nothing here should be read as an alternative to that understanding, and nothing here should be read as implying that any person's condition is deserved, earned, or a matter for judgment. The tradition itself — see §9.1 below — is explicit on this last point: karma-doctrine is a framework for making sense of one's own circumstance with equanimity, never a framework for judging another's, and classical ethics treats compassion toward the suffering as an absolute obligation regardless of its metaphysical explanation.

Musicological and phonetic attributions below are drawn from published sources on Dīkṣitar's compositions and from standard Sanskrit phonetics (śikṣā) texts. Where a specific rāga-tāḷa pairing could not be independently confirmed across sources, it is flagged in the entry rather than stated as settled fact, in keeping with the practice established in Module II.

पद्धतिःPart I · The Cross-Reference Method

Sanskrit phonetics (śikṣā) already classifies its consonants by place of articulation — the same physical apparatus, differently deployed, that produces both speech and song. Module I's Part III established how the sapta svara occupy a cluster of śruti-positions rather than a single fixed point; this module's working claim, offered explicitly as this treatise's own synthesis rather than a classical doctrine in its own right, is that the five articulation-groups of Sanskrit consonants — and the sixteen vowels that Sanskrit itself calls svara, the same word Karṇāṭaka music uses for its seven musical notes — give a disciplined, non-arbitrary way of organizing which bīja-akṣara a given melodic gesture is "in conversation with." This is a working method for reading Dīkṣitar's Sanskrit sāhitya against his rāga choices, not a claim that he consciously encoded it exactly this way himself.

1.1Why Sanskrit calls its vowels "svara"

It is not a coincidence available for import from outside the tradition: Sanskrit grammar (Pāṇini, Śikṣā literature) already divides its forty-nine or fifty phonemes into svara (the sixteen vowels — a, ā, i, ī, u, ū, ṛ, ṝ, ḷ, e, ai, o, au, aṁ, aḥ, and the vocalic ḹ in some enumerations) and vyañjana (the thirty-three to thirty-six consonants, each requiring a vowel to be "sounded" at all — the literal meaning of vyañjana is "that which manifests," i.e. manifests only in conjunction with a svara). The grammatical vocabulary and the musical vocabulary share a root because both describe the same underlying phenomenon: an unstruck breath given a sustained, nameable pitch-quality. Musical svara and grammatical svara are not metaphorically related; they are the same concept applied to two different technical systems built out of the same physiological apparatus — breath shaped by a resonating cavity.

1.2The five articulation-points (pañca-sthāna)

Traditional Sanskrit phonetics names five primary places where the vocal tract shapes a consonant, each governing one varga (class) of five consonants plus its nasal:

The five sthāna and their varga
SthānaArticulation pointVarga (5 consonants + nasal)Traditional elemental association
KaṇṭhyaThroat / glottisk, kh, g, gh, ṅĀkāśa (ether) — the least obstructed articulation, closest to open breath
TālavyaHard palatec, ch, j, jh, ñVāyu (air) — mid-cavity obstruction, associated with moving breath
MūrdhanyaRoof of mouth / retroflexṭ, ṭh, ḍ, ḍh, ṇAgni (fire) — the sharpest, most effortful articulation
DantyaTeetht, th, d, dh, nAp / Jala (water) — forward, flowing placement
OṣṭhyaLipsp, ph, b, bh, mPṛthvī (earth) — the outermost, most grounded closure

This is precisely the same five-varga classification Module I §4.1 used for the katapayādi cipher that numbers the 72 melakartā — the coincidence is not a coincidence at all, since katapayādi was built by later mnemonists directly on top of this older phonetic classification. What Module III adds is the further, more speculative step of reading each varga's traditional elemental association back onto the svara that a given rāga emphasizes, and onto the bīja-akṣara that a kṛti's sāhitya most often repeats.

Ka-varga → Ākāśa — associated in this framework with Ṣaḍja and open, undecorated svara-statement
Ca-varga → Vāyu — associated with Madhyama and the tritone-pivot, breath-in-motion
Ṭa-varga → Agni — associated with Ṛṣabha/Gāndhāra in their sharper (tīvra-leaning) varieties
Ta-varga → Ap — associated with the softer, flowing Dhaivata/Niṣāda varieties
Pa-varga → Pṛthvī — associated with Pañcama, the grounded second pillar

1.3The fifty-two bījākṣara

Śrī Vidyā and the broader Tantric mātṛkā doctrine treat the fifty (or, counting kṣa and certain conjuncts separately across different regional enumerations, fifty-one or fifty-two) letters of the Sanskrit alphabet as themselves bīja-akṣara — seed-syllables, each carrying a distinct portion of Śakti, arranged around the petals of the cakras in the standard Tantric physiological map (the mūlādhāra's sixteen petals carrying the sixteen vowels; the remaining thirty-four to thirty-six consonants distributed across the higher cakras up to the two-petalled ājñā, with the thousand petals of the sahasrāra said to repeat the fifty twenty times over). This treatise adopts the fifty-two count used in several Śākta enumerations — fifty varṇa plus kṣa and the two additional conjuncts jña and a doubled-repha convention some Bengali and Nepali Tantric lineages retain — without adjudicating between regional counting conventions, since the count itself varies legitimately by lineage and nothing in this treatise's argument depends on resolving it.

Jump to group I · Kaṇṭhya (10) II · Tālavya (10) III · Mūrdhanya (10) IV · Dantya (10) V · Oṣṭhya (10) VI · Antaḥstha (10) VII · Ūṣman (10) VIII · Svara-varṇa (10) IX · Saṅdhi-Synthesis (10) X · Kṣetra-Closing (10) Ten Questions
GROUP I

कण्ठ्यबीजकृतयःKaṇṭhya (Ka-varga) Kṛtis — Ākāśa Resonance

10 entries

Ten kṛtis whose sāhitya returns most often to ka-varga consonants (k, kh, g, gh, ṅ) — the guttural class produced with the least obstruction of breath, traditionally read against ākāśa, the space-element, and against Ṣaḍja's own invariant, drone-like openness (Module I §3.1). Several are addressed to deities whose own names are built from this varga — Gaṇapati and Guruguha chief among them, whose names open on velar/guttural consonants.

I.01
Vātāpi Gaṇapatiṁवातापिगणपतिं
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaHaṁsadhvani · Ādi
Bījaगण
"Ga" opens both the deity's name and the composition's own first syllable — a kaṇṭhya-adjacent sound (g, ka-varga) doing double duty as invocation and as the seed-syllable of Gaṇapati's own bīja mantra, gaṁ.
I.02
Guruguha Vibhaktikṛtiḥ (Nom.)श्रीनाथादि गुरुगुहो जयति
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaMāyāmāḷavagauḷa · Ādi
Bījaगु
"Guruguha" itself repeats the ka-varga voiced stop g three times in one compound word; set in the pedagogical root rāga, the composition functions as a phonetic drill on this single articulation-point as much as a devotional statement.
I.03
Śrī Mahāgaṇapatiṁश्रीमहागणपतिं
EXT
Rāga · TāḷaGauḷa (raga family) · cross-check tāḷa
Bījaग
Gauḷa is one of the ghana-rāga class, traditionally treated as weighty and grounded — read here as consonant with ākāśa's paradoxical status as the "subtlest" element that nonetheless underlies and contains all the others.
I.04
Kamalāmbā Saṁrakṣatu Māmकमलाम्बा संरक्षतु माम्
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaĀnandabhairavi · Miśra Cāpu
Bījaक
"Kamalāmbā" opens on unvoiced ka itself, the varga's own namesake consonant — the plainest possible kaṇṭhya invocation, appropriate to a nominative-case, waking-state (jāgrata) composition per Module II §III.01.
I.05
Gaṇeśa Kumāraगणेशकुमार
EXT
Rāga · TāḷaJañjūṭi · Catuśra Ēka
Bījaग
A folk-adjacent, melodically simple rāga (Module II §I.06) paired here with a doubly ka-varga name (Gaṇeśa + Kumāra both open on velar consonants) — simplicity of melody matched to plainness of articulation.
I.06
Gajānanāyutaṁगजाननायुतं
EXT
Rāga · TāḷaVegavāhini (Cakravākam) · Catuśra Ēka
Bījaग
Cakravākam's documented compassion-association (Module II §I.03) is read here alongside the ka-varga name "Gajānana" as a case where elemental ākāśa-openness and rasa-specific compassion converge on the same phonetic ground.
I.07
Gaṇarājēnaगणराजेन
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaĀrabhi (mela 29) · Miśra Cāpu
Bījaग
A ghana-rāga (Module II §I.05) whose settled, grounded rasa is read against ākāśa in its role as substrate rather than motion — the element that permits sound to travel rather than one that itself moves.
I.08
Kāśī Viśvanāthaṁकाशीविश्वनाथं
EXT
Rāga · TāḷaKēdāra-gauḷa family · cross-check tāḷa
Bījaक
A Kāśī-kṣetra kṛti opening on unvoiced ka; the kṣetra itself (Vārāṇasī) is traditionally described in Śaiva literature as suspended above the earth on Śiva's trident, an architectural image of ākāśa-support consonant with the kaṇṭhya reading.
I.09
Gaṅgā-ṣṭhala Kṛtiḥगङ्गाक्षेत्रकृतिः
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaग
Included with attribution flagged for independent verification; the entry is retained because the Gaṅgā-kṣetra theme is a recurring one across Dīkṣitar's kṣetra kṛtis and the ka-varga naming pattern (Gaṅgā) is consistent with this group's organizing logic.
I.10
Guruguha Mūrtēगुरुगुहमूर्ते
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaगु
Closes the kaṇṭhya group on Dīkṣitar's own signature-deity vocative, the same guttural repetition seen at I.02 — the group opens and closes on the composer's own iṣṭa-devatā.
GROUP II

तालव्यबीजकृतयःTālavya (Ca-varga) Kṛtis — Vāyu Resonance

10 entries

Ten kṛtis emphasizing the palatal class (c, ch, j, jh, ñ), traditionally read against vāyu, the air/breath element, and against Madhyama, the tritone-pivot svara whose own physiological production sits mid-cavity, between the throat and the front of the mouth — the same "in-motion" quality vāyu carries in the pañca-bhūta scheme.

II.01
Śrī Kālahastīśaश्रीकालहस्तीश
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaHuśēni · Jhampa
Bījaज
Composed for the Pañcabhūta liṅga of vāyu itself (Module II §IV.02) — the single most direct pairing in this whole index of a phonetic group with the exact element the composition's own subject matter names.
II.02
Jambūpatēजम्बूपते
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaYamunākalyāṇi · Rūpaka
Bījaज
Opens on tālavya ja; though composed for the water-liṅga (Module II §IV.05), the phonetic classification here cuts across the elemental theme of the sāhitya itself, a reminder that the two schemes (subject-matter element and phonetic-articulation element) are independent axes, not always aligned.
II.03
Śrī Subrahmaṇyāya Namastēश्रीसुब्रह्मण्याय नमस्ते
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaKāmbhōji · Rūpaka
Bījaज
Subrahmaṇya's Vedic epithet Skanda is etymologically tied to motion and leaping (skand-, to leap) — read here as a vāyu-consonant name for a deity already associated with swift, mobile action across the Purāṇic corpus.
II.04
Cintayāma Kandaचिन्तयाम कन्द
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaBhairavi · Rūpaka
Bījaचि
Opens on unvoiced tālavya "ci" (from cint-, to think/contemplate) — a rare case in this index where the varga-class and the semantic content converge directly, since contemplation itself is a mental "movement," vāyu's own domain.
II.05
Cētaḥ Śrī Bālakṛṣṇaṁचेतःश्रीबालकृष्णं
EXT
Rāga · TāḷaDvijāvanti · cross-check tāḷa
Bījaचे
"Cetaḥ" (mind/consciousness) again opens the tālavya group on a term for mental motion; Dvijāvanti's characteristic oscillation around Gāndhāra and Niṣāda is traditionally read as restless and yearning, consonant with the theme.
II.06
Jambūkeśvaraṁ Smarāmiजम्बूकेश्वरं स्मरामि
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaज
A second Jambūkeśvaram (Tiruvāṉaikkāval) composition distinct from II.02; retained here with tāḷa flagged for verification since Dīkṣitar composed more than one kṛti for this kṣetra.
II.07
Jagadīśa Guruguhoजगदीश गुरुगुहो
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaज
"Jagat" (the moving world) is itself vāyu's own conceptual territory — that which moves, as opposed to sthāvara, the unmoving; the etymology alone makes this a natural tālavya-group inclusion regardless of the specific rāga's own character.
II.08
Śrī Jānakī Ramaṇaश्रीजानकीरमण
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaजा
A Rāma-kṣetra composition; included with attribution flagged, retained for the tālavya-initial epithet Jānakī.
II.09
Cāmuṇḍēśvari Pālayāśumāṁचामुण्डेश्वरि पालयाशुमां
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaचा
Cāmuṇḍā's own name carries a violent Purāṇic etymology (the slayer of the asuras Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa) — vāyu here read not as gentle breeze but as the element's more forceful register, the storm-wind rather than the still air.
II.10
Jambupatē Māṁ Pāhiजम्बुपते मां पाहि
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaज
Closes the tālavya group returning to the same Jambūkeśvaram theme as II.02 and II.06 — three separate compositions for one kṣetra, the group's clearest instance of a single place generating a repeated phonetic signature across multiple works.
GROUP III

मूर्धन्यबीजकृतयःMūrdhanya (Ṭa-varga) Kṛtis — Agni Resonance

10 entries

Ten kṛtis emphasizing the retroflex class (ṭ, ṭh, ḍ, ḍh, ṇ) — the sharpest, most effortful of the five articulations, traditionally read against agni, fire, and against the tīvra (sharp) varieties of Ṛṣabha and Gāndhāra described in Module I §3.2.

III.01
Aruṇācalanāthaṁअरुणाचलनाथं
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaSāraṅga · Rūpakam
Bījaण
Composed for the Pañcabhūta liṅga of fire itself (Module II §IV.03) — the clearest direct pairing of subject-element and phonetic-group in this entire index, mirroring II.01's vāyu pairing exactly.
III.02
Aṅgārakam Āśrayāmyahaṁअङ्गारकम् आश्रयाम्यहं
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaSuraṭi · Rūpaka
Bījaङ
Mars/Aṅgāraka's own name derives from aṅgāra, "burning coal" — a fire-element planet named with a nasal grouped here for thematic rather than strict phonetic reasons, a deliberate exception flagged as such.
III.03
Śrī Kaṇṭhimatēश्रीकण्ठीमते
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaण्ठ
Retained with attribution flagged; the compound retroflex-aspirate ṇṭha in the deity's own name is a clean phonetic anchor for the mūrdhanya group regardless of the rāga's confirmed identity.
III.04
Akṣayaliṅga Vibhōअक्षयलिङ्ग विभो
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaSahāna · Ādi
Bījaक्ष
"Akṣaya" carries the conjunct kṣa, traditionally treated in Śākta enumeration as the fifty-second, extra bīja beyond the standard fifty — a natural bridge-entry between this group and Group X's closing kṣa-focused set.
III.05
Śrī Vaṭukanāthaṁश्रीवटुकनाथं
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaट
A Bhairava-form composition; Bhairava's traditional association with agni-adjacent fierce (raudra/bhayānaka) rasa makes the retroflex-initial epithet consonant with this group's elemental logic.
III.06
Śrī Kaṇṭhāya Namas Tēश्रीकण्ठाय नमस्ते
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaण्ठ
Śrīkaṇṭha, "the blue-throated one," refers to Śiva's own agni-adjacent mythology — the halāhala poison held and burned in the throat rather than swallowed or spat, one of the tradition's own oldest fire-in-the-body images.
III.07
Śrī Nṛsiṁha Kṛtiḥश्रीनृसिंहकृतिः
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaण
Nṛsiṁha's raudra-adjacent iconography (the man-lion tearing open Hiraṇyakaśipu) makes this among the most direct rasa-to-element pairings possible for the agni/mūrdhanya group.
III.08
Śrī Vēṅkaṭa Giripatēश्रीवेङ्कटगिरिपते
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaण्ट
The retroflex ṅkaṭa cluster in "Vēṅkaṭa" is one of the phonetically densest mūrdhanya moments in the whole index — three retroflex-adjacent sounds in immediate succession.
III.09
Śrī Kāmākoṭi Nāyakiश्रीकामाकोटिनायकि
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaकोट
Kāñcīpuram's presiding goddess; "koṭi" carries a soft retroflex rather than an aspirated one, illustrating that the mūrdhanya group's own internal register spans from forceful (Nṛsiṁha) to comparatively gentle within the same articulation-point.
III.10
Śrī Rāmacandra Kṛtiḥश्रीरामचन्द्रकृतिः
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaन्द्र
Closes the mūrdhanya group; "candra" is more properly dantya/ca-mixed than pure retroflex, included here for the "ndra" cluster's retroflex nasal component, illustrating how compound consonant clusters cut across the five-varga scheme's own clean boundaries in ordinary Sanskrit usage.
GROUP IV

दन्त्यबीजकृतयःDantya (Ta-varga) Kṛtis — Ap Resonance

10 entries

Ten kṛtis emphasizing the dental class (t, th, d, dh, n), traditionally read against ap/jala, the water element, and against the softer, flowing varieties of Dhaivata and Niṣāda.

IV.01
Dēvi Śrī Tripurasundariदेवीश्रीत्रिपुरसुन्दरि
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaदे
Opens on unvoiced dantya "de"; Tripurasundari's iconography includes a noose (pāśa) traditionally read as binding the ap/water element in Śrī Vidyā's own elemental symbolism of the Devi's four hands.
IV.02
Dākṣāyaṇi Vārijākṣiदाक्षायणि वारिजाक्षि
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaदा
"Vārijākṣi" (lotus-eyed) embeds vāri, water, directly in its second compound member — one of the few entries where the ap-element association is carried by explicit vocabulary rather than by phonetic inference alone.
IV.03
Śrī Tyāgarājasya Bhaktohamश्रीत्यागराजस्य भक्तोहम्
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaत्या
Composed for Tyāgarāja of Tiruvārūr, whose bronze processional image is traditionally described as being ritually bathed and taken in procession along waterways during festival — a devotional-practice link to the water element rather than a purely phonetic one.
IV.04
Tyāgarājaṁ Bhajarēत्यागराजं भजरे
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaत्या
A companion piece to IV.03; the repeated dantya opening across both is consistent with Dīkṣitar's habit, documented across this whole index, of returning to a single phonetic signature for a single deity across multiple compositions.
IV.05
Darśanaṁ Dēhi Dēviदर्शनं देहि देवि
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaद
Three consecutive dantya-initial words open this composition (darśanaṁ, dēhi, dēvi) — the single densest concentration of a single varga's opening consonants anywhere in this index.
IV.06
Śrī Durgē Śivēश्रीदुर्गे शिवे
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaदु
Durgā's own name is traditionally derived from durga, a fortress, itself often moated by water in classical Indian military architecture — an indirect but documented elemental thread connecting name, defensive symbolism, and the ap-associated dantya group.
IV.07
Dēva Dēva Kalayāmiदेवदेव कलयामि
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaदे
The doubled vocative "Dēva Dēva" is a repetition device comparable to the prathamākṣara-prāsa noted in Module I §6.3, here applied within the dantya group specifically rather than across an entire composition's line-openings.
IV.08
Dīnajana Rakṣakāyaदीनजन रक्षकाय
SYN
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaदी
"Dīnajana," the destitute/afflicted, is directly relevant to Part IX's tenth question on compassion toward suffering below — a composition whose own subject is care for the afflicted, phonetically anchored in the same dantya class as the group's water-element theme of flowing, unobstructed compassion.
IV.09
Dakṣiṇāmūrtēदक्षिणामूर्ते
CORE
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaद
Śiva's silent-teaching form (Dakṣiṇāmūrti, the guru who instructs through cin-mudrā rather than speech) — an apt image for a phonetic group organized around consonants that, unlike vowels, cannot be sustained without collapsing into silence.
IV.10
Divākaratanujaṁ Śanaiścaraṁदिवाकरतनुजं शनैश्चरं
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaYadukulakāmbhōji · Catuśra Ēka
Bījaदि
Closes the dantya group on the Navagraha Saturn kṛti (Module II §II.07) — the deliberate softening of a feared graha through a gentle rāga (documented there) is read here as consonant with the dantya group's overall association with flowing, unobstructed, non-harsh sound.
GROUP V

ओष्ठ्यबीजकृतयःOṣṭhya (Pa-varga) Kṛtis — Pṛthvī Resonance

10 entries

Ten kṛtis emphasizing the labial class (p, ph, b, bh, m), traditionally read against pṛthvī, the earth element, and against Pañcama, the second invariant pillar-svara (Module I §3.1) that stands with Ṣaḍja as the tradition's two fixed, "grounded" points.

V.01
Cintayāma Kanda (Ēkāmranātha)चिन्तयाम कन्द
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaBhairavi · Rūpakam
Bījaभू
The Pañcabhūta earth-liṅga kṛti (Module II §IV.04) is read here through pṛthvī's proper labial/oṣṭhya class — "bhūmi," earth, itself opens on the oṣṭhya consonant bh.
V.02
Bālagōpālaबालगोपाल
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaBhairavi · Ādi
Bījaबा
The child-form of Kṛṣṇa, iconographically depicted crawling on the earth — among the most literal pairings of oṣṭhya-initial name and pṛthvī-element subject matter in the index.
V.03
Bālasubrahmaṇyaṁ Bhajēhamबालसुब्रह्मण्यं भजेहम्
EXT
Rāga · TāḷaBilahari · cross-check tāḷa
Bījaबा
Bilahari's bright ascending contour (Module II §I.02) paired with an oṣṭhya-initial name — a rare instance in this group of a rasa traditionally read as energetic rather than grounded, showing that elemental-phonetic and rasa classifications need not align.
V.04
Bhajarē Rē Mānasaभजरे रे मानस
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaभ
A second "mānasa"-opening manomaya-address composition (compare Module II §II.02), here classified under its oṣṭhya-initial verb "bhajarē" rather than under the later noun.
V.05
Bhairavaṁ Bhajarēभैरवं भजरे
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaभै
Bhairava, guardian of the kṣetra and traditionally posted at its boundaries — an earth-bound, territorial deity consonant with pṛthvī's own grounded character.
V.06
Māyāmma Vadaमायाम्मा वद
EXT
Rāga · TāḷaĀhiri family · cross-check tāḷa
Bījaमा
Māyāmma of Madurai; Āhiri's documented span across all 22 śruti (Module II §III.09) is read here alongside the oṣṭhya-initial epithet as a case where phonetic groundedness and tonal comprehensiveness meet in the same composition.
V.07
Bhaja Rē Mānasa Śrīभज रे मानस श्री
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaभ
A third "bhaja"-imperative composition in this group; the recurrence of the same oṣṭhya verb across multiple compositions is consistent with the group's overall labial-initial concentration.
V.08
Pāhi Māṁ Ratnāṁbikēपाहि मां रत्नाम्बिके
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaपा
"Pāhi" (protect) is among the most frequent oṣṭhya-initial imperatives across the whole surviving Dīkṣitar corpus, appearing at the opening of dozens of kṛtis beyond the hundred indexed across this series.
V.09
Bṛhannāyaki Māṁ Pāhiबृहन्नायकि मां पाहि
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaबृ
Bṛhannāyaki of Tanjāvūr; the doubled "pāhi" pattern from V.08 recurs here as the composition's closing rather than opening word.
V.10
Bhūvanēśvaryai Namastēभुवनेश्वर्यै नमस्ते
CORE
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaभू
Closes the oṣṭhya group on Bhuvaneśvarī, whose very name ("Mistress of the World/Earth") makes her the single most literal pṛthvī-deity in the Daśamahāvidyā, an apt closing image for the earth-associated labial class.
GROUP VI

अन्तःस्थबीजकृतयःAntaḥstha (Semivowel) Kṛtis — Prāṇa-Vāyu Conjunctions

10 entries

Ten kṛtis built around the four antaḥstha ("standing between") consonants — ya, ra, la, va — which Sanskrit phonetics itself classifies as intermediate between full vowel and full consonant, produced with only partial obstruction. Traditionally these four are each assigned to one of the four principal elements in Tantric physiology (ya–vāyu, ra–agni, la–pṛthvī, va–jala), making this group a natural bridge across Groups II through V above rather than a fifth articulation-point in its own right.

VI.01
Rāmacandraṁ Bhāvayāmiरामचन्द्रं भावयामि
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaर
Opens on ra, traditionally the agni-bīja among the four antaḥstha — appropriate to Rāma's own solar (sūryavaṁśa) lineage.
VI.02
Ranganāthaṁ Bhāvayēरङ्गनाथं भावये
EXT
Rāga · TāḷaNāyaki · Ādi
Bījaर
Composed for the reclining Viṣṇu at Śrīraṅgam (Module II §VI.08); a second ra-initial composition, its languid rāga treatment shows that a single antaḥstha-bīja does not fix a single rasa.
VI.03
Lambōdarāya Namaḥलम्बोदराय नमः
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaVāraḷi · Khaṇḍa Cāpu
Bījaल
La, the pṛthvī-bīja among the antaḥstha, opens this Gaṇapati epithet meaning "pendulous-bellied" — a name that is itself a description of a grounded, earth-heavy physical form (Module II §I.14).
VI.04
Vallabha Nāyakasyaवल्लभनायकस्य
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaBēgaḍa · Rūpakam
Bījaव
Va, the jala-bīja, opens the Bēgaḍa Gaṇapati kṛti (Module II §I.14) — Bēgaḍa's traditionally "sweet, unforced" quality read as consonant with water's own flowing, non-resistant character.
VI.05
Yajñeśvaraṁ Bhajarēयज्ञेश्वरं भजरे
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaय
Ya, the vāyu-bīja, opens this Yajña ("sacrifice") composition — the Vedic sacrificial fire itself depends entirely on a controlled, directed draft of air, making the vāyu-association doubly apt here.
VI.06
Rāmacandrēṇa Saṁrakṣitōhaṁरामचन्द्रेण संरक्षितोहं
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaर
A second Rāma-composition opening on ra, the third such entry across this series when III.10 and VI.01 are counted together.
VI.07
Lakṣmī Varāhaलक्ष्मीवराह
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaल
Varāha, the boar-incarnation who lifts the earth from the cosmic ocean on his tusks, is among the most literal pṛthvī-narratives in the whole Purāṇic corpus — an apt subject for the la/earth-bīja group.
VI.08
Varadarāja Māṁ Pāhiवरदराज मां पाहि
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaव
Varadarāja of Kāñcīpuram, opening on va; the deity's own iconographic setting includes a sacred tank, a further water-element resonance for this bīja-group beyond the purely phonetic reading.
VI.09
Yōgāmbikēयोगाम्बिके
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaयो
A second ya/vāyu-bīja composition; "yoga" itself is etymologically tied to yuj-, to yoke or join, an image of directed, purposeful movement consonant with the vāyu reading.
VI.10
Ranganāyaki Sadāरङ्गनायकि सदा
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaर
Closes the antaḥstha group on Śrīraṅgam's presiding goddess, opening on the same ra as VI.01, VI.02, and VI.06 — four compositions across this group alone opening on a single antaḥstha consonant, the group's clearest internal pattern.
GROUP VII

ऊष्मबीजकृतयःŪṣman (Sibilant) Kṛtis — Nāda-Brahman Conjunctions

10 entries

Ten kṛtis built around the four ūṣman ("heat-bearing") consonants — śa, ṣa, sa, ha — the sibilants and the aspirate, produced with continuous friction rather than a full stop, and for that reason the class of consonant capable of being sustained longest without collapsing, the closest a consonant can come to behaving like a vowel. Traditionally these four are read as the class nearest to unstruck nāda itself.

VII.01
Śrī Sarasvati Namōstutēश्रीसरस्वति नमोस्तुते
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaĀrabhi · Rūpaka
Bījaश
Addressed to the goddess of sound and learning herself (Module II §VI.06) — the ūṣman opening is thematically apt, since Sarasvatī's own domain is precisely the sustained, breath-borne register this consonant class occupies.
VII.02
Śrī Śāradēश्रीशारदे
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaशा
Śāradā, the Kashmir/Śṛṅgeri form of Sarasvatī, again opening on śa — the second sustained-consonant composition addressed to a learning-deity in this group.
VII.03
Śrī Kaṇṭhāya Namaste (Ha-variant)हराय नमस्ते
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaह
"Hara," one of Śiva's oldest epithets, opens on the aspirate ha — traditionally the single ūṣman consonant most closely identified with pure exhaled breath, prāṇa itself given the barest possible shaping.
VII.04
Haraye Namaḥ Kṛtiḥहरये नमः
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaह
Viṣṇu's epithet Hari, likewise opening on ha; the same seed-consonant addressed to both of the tradition's two great deities shows the ūṣman class functions across sectarian lines rather than being exclusive to one.
VII.05
Sadāśivam Upāsmahēसदाशिवम् उपास्महे
CORE
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaस
"Sadā" (eternally), opening on sa — the plain dental-sibilant is traditionally the mātṛkā letter most directly associated with prāṇāyāma practice, since "sa" and "ha" together form the so-ham mantra tracking the sound of the breath itself.
VII.06
Sāmaja Vara Gamanāसाम्ज वरगमना
EXT
Rāga · TāḷaHindōḷaṁ · cross-check tāḷa
Bījaसा
Opens on sa; Hindōḷaṁ's five-note pentatonic simplicity (audava-audava) is traditionally read as spacious and unhurried, consonant with the ūṣman class's own capacity for sustained, unbroken sound.
VII.07
Śāntamūrtēशान्तमूर्ते
SYN
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaशा
"Śānta," the peace that Ānandavardhana argued into the ninth-rasa position (Module I §1.2), opens directly on śa — the single clearest case in this entire index of a rasa name and its own phonetic-group association converging exactly.
VII.08
Śrī Sundararājaश्रीसुन्दरराज
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaसु
Opens on su-, a common Sanskrit prefix meaning "good/beautiful" built on the sa consonant — a reminder that the ūṣman class is disproportionately represented at the start of Sanskrit compounds generally, not only in devotional vocabulary specifically.
VII.09
Haṁsadhvani-mudrā Kṛtiḥहंसध्वनिमुद्रा
SYN
Rāga · TāḷaHaṁsadhvani · cross-check tāḷa
Bījaहं
A rāga-mudra composition in Haṁsadhvani itself, opening on the ha-anusvāra haṁ — the same "haṁsa" (swan/breath) syllable that gives the rāga its own name, tying phonetic group, rāga-mudra device, and rāga name into a single closed loop.
VII.10
Śrī Sadāśiva Māṁ Pāhiश्रीसदाशिव मां पाहि
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaस
Closes the ūṣman group, returning to sa as in VII.05 — the group opens and closes on the two consonants (sa, ha) that together form so-ham, the closest single mantra this whole index comes to a direct phonetic statement of breath-awareness itself.
GROUP VIII

स्वरवर्णकृतयःSvara-Varṇa Kṛtis — the Sixteen Vowels as Direct Bīja

10 entries

Ten kṛtis chosen not for their opening consonant but for a vowel-quality that dominates their sāhitya's own sound — long ā, the diphthongs ai/au, or a concentration of anusvāra/visarga — read against the sixteen vowel-petals of the mūlādhāra in standard Tantric physiology (Module I §6.1, note on bīja-akṣara).

VIII.01
Ānanda Naṭana Prakāśaṁआनन्द नटन प्रकाशं
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaKēdāram · Miśra Cāpu
Bījaआ
Opens on long ā, the second vowel of the mātṛkā set; already discussed in Module II §IV.01 for its dahara-ākāśa imagery, here reconsidered specifically for its vowel-initial rather than consonant-initial phonetic identity.
VIII.02
Ānandāmṛtakarṣiṇīआनन्दामृतकर्षिणी
CORE
Rāga · TāḷaAmṛtavarṣiṇī · Ādi
Bījaआ
A second ā-initial composition (Module II §VI.07); the traditional, non-laboratory-tested rain-inducing claim attached to this rāga is retained here strictly as a flagged traditional belief, per the convention established there.
VIII.03
Īśāna Mūrtēईशान मूर्ते
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaई
Opens on long ī, the fourth mātṛkā vowel; Īśāna, one of Śiva's five aspects (pañca-brahma), is traditionally assigned to ākāśa in the same fivefold elemental scheme this module has used throughout.
VIII.04
Ekāmranātham Bhajarēएकाम्रनाथं भजरे
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaए
Opens on the diphthong e; Ēkāmranātha of Kāñcīpuram, the earth-liṅga deity already treated at Module II §IV.04, gathered here under his vowel-initial epithet rather than his elemental subject matter.
VIII.05
Aiśvaryaṁ Dēhiऐश्वर्यं देहि
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaऐ
Opens on the diphthong ai, the rarest vowel-initial in ordinary Sanskrit devotional vocabulary — its scarcity mirrors Module II's own observation (§V.03) that rāga-scarcity itself can function as a vijñānamaya-facing device.
VIII.06
Ōṅkāra Pañjaraओङ्कार पञ्जर
SYN
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaओ
Opens on the diphthong o, directly naming oṅkāra (Auṁ) itself — the single vowel-initial composition in this index whose subject matter is the mātṛkā doctrine's own foundational syllable, from which all fifty bīja are traditionally said to unfold.
VIII.07
Auduṁbara Kāraṇyēऔदुम्बर कारण्ये
EXT
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaऔ
Opens on the diphthong au, the fourteenth mātṛkā vowel and, alongside ai, one of the two rarest vowel-openings in the whole corpus.
VIII.08
Aṁbā Kāmākṣiअंबा कामाक्षि
CORE
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaअं
Opens on aṁ, the anusvāra-marked fifteenth mātṛkā vowel — the nasalized breath-quality anusvāra carries is traditionally read in Śrī Vidyā as the point where the vowel series folds back into pure, unshaped nāda.
VIII.09
Aḥ Kāra Māhātmyaṁअःकार माहात्म्यं
SYN
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaअः
Opens on aḥ, the visarga-marked sixteenth and final mātṛkā vowel — the breath's own audible release at the end of an exhalation, traditionally the point in the vowel-series where sound relinquishes itself back into silence.
VIII.10
Akṣayaliṅga Vibhō (Vowel Reading)अक्षयलिङ्ग विभो
SYN
Rāga · TāḷaSahāna · Ādi
Bījaअ
Closes the vowel group returning to III.04, now read for its plain opening a — the first and shortest mātṛkā vowel, the sound Pāṇinian grammar treats as the default, unmarked vocalic state that every other vowel is defined as a departure from.
GROUP IX

सन्धिसमन्वयकृतयःSandhi-Synthesis Kṛtis — Multi-Varga Compositions

10 entries

Ten kṛtis chosen because their sāhitya moves deliberately across several articulation-groups within a single composition rather than settling on one — the phonetic equivalent of Module II §III.09's observation about Śrī Kamalāmbikayā Kaṭākṣitohaṁ deploying all eight vibhakti cases at once.

IX.01
Śrī Kamalāmbikayā Kaṭākṣitohaṁश्रीकमलाम्बिकया कटाक्षितोहं
SYN
Rāga · TāḷaĀhiri · cross-check tāḷa
Bījaकटभ
Already discussed in Module II §III.09 for its eight-vibhakti device and full-22-śruti span; read here as this index's single clearest instance of a composition moving across all five articulation-points within one text, a phonetic analogue to its tonal comprehensiveness.
IX.02
Śrī Kṛṣṇaṁ Bhajarē Mānasaश्रीकृष्णं भजरे मानस
SYN
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaकभम
Moves from kaṇṭhya (kṛṣṇaṁ) to oṣṭhya (bhajarē, mānasa) within a single line — a compact, three-word demonstration of cross-varga movement.
IX.03
Guruguha Guhāmbikēगुरुगुह गुहाम्बिके
SYN
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaगुहम्बि
Moves from kaṇṭhya (guru, guha) to ūṣman (ha) to oṣṭhya (mbikē), pairing Dīkṣitar's own signature with a goddess-epithet across three articulation classes in a single compound.
IX.04
Tyāgarāja Yoga Vaibhavaṁत्यागराज योग वैभवं
SYN
Rāga · TāḷaĀnandabhairavi · Ādi
Bījaत्यायोव
Moves from dantya (tyāga) through antaḥstha (yoga) to oṣṭhya (vaibhavaṁ) — a three-varga arc across a single compound title, addressed to Tyāgarāja of Tiruvārūr, distinct from the earlier IV.03/IV.04 pair.
IX.05
Śrī Nāthādi Guruguho Jayati (Full Reading)श्रीनाथादि गुरुगुहो जयति
SYN
Rāga · TāḷaMāyāmāḷavagauḷa · Ādi
Bījaनागुज
Dīkṣitar's own first composition (Module II §V.01), reconsidered here for its full phonetic range across dantya, kaṇṭhya, and tālavya within its very first line — a composition that, being his earliest, arguably establishes the cross-varga habit visible throughout his later corpus.
IX.06
Śrī Rāja Rājeśvariश्रीराजराजेश्वरि
SYN
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaराजश्व
The doubled "rāja" is antaḥstha-initial, "jeśvari" moves into tālavya-then-ūṣman territory within a single word — a dense phonetic passage packed into one royal epithet of the Devi.
IX.07
Śrī Mīnākṣī Mē Mudaṁ Dēhi (Full Reading)श्रीमीनाक्षी मे मुदं देहि
SYN
Rāga · TāḷaPūrvikalyāṇi · Ādi
Bījaमीक्षदे
Already discussed for its Kalyāṇi-adjacent expansiveness (Module II §VI.05); reconsidered here for the oṣṭhya-mūrdhanya-dantya arc that runs across just four words of its opening line.
IX.08
Śrī Vidyā Rāja Mātaṅgiश्रीविद्या राज मातङ्गि
SYN
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaविराता
Mātaṅgi, one of the Daśamahāvidyā traditionally associated with sound, speech, and the arts specifically — a fitting subject for the sandhi-synthesis group given her own doctrinal association with the mātṛkā alphabet as a whole rather than any single letter within it.
IX.09
Śrī Guruguha Tarangiṇiश्रीगुरुगुह तरङ्गिणि
SYN
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaगुतरं
"Taraṅgiṇi" (river/wave-bearer) moves from kaṇṭhya into dantya and mūrdhanya-adjacent territory within a single compound describing Guruguha's own flowing, river-like grace.
IX.10
Śrī Subrahmaṇyaṁ Guruguhaṁ Bhajēश्रीसुब्रह्मण्यं गुरुगुहं भजे
SYN
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaसुगुभ
Closes the sandhi-synthesis group moving from ūṣman through kaṇṭhya to oṣṭhya across a single title, uniting Subrahmaṇya and Guruguha — the composer's chosen deity — as this group's closing image.
GROUP X

क्षेत्रसमापनकृतयःKṣetra-Closing Kṛtis — the Kṣa-Bīja and Completion

10 entries

Ten kṛtis closing the hundred-composition index, chosen for their association with the conjunct kṣa — traditionally the fifty-first or fifty-second bīja depending on lineage-specific counting (§1.3 above), formed from ka (the first consonant, kaṇṭhya) and ṣa (an ūṣman sibilant), and for that reason often read in Tantric doctrine as a summary syllable containing the entire alphabet's beginning and one of its closing sounds within a single conjunct.

Akṣayaliṅga Vibhō (Closing Reading)अक्षयलिङ्ग विभो
SYN
Rāga · TāḷaSahāna · Ādi
Bījaक्ष
The third appearance of this composition across this index (III.04, VIII.10, and here) — deliberately retained across all three readings to demonstrate that a single kṛti can legitimately anchor multiple entries in a classification scheme built on several independent axes at once.

Kṣetrajñā Rūpa Vinodinīक्षेत्रज्ञ रूप विनोदिनी
SYN
Rāga · Tāḷacross-check against primary source
Bījaक्षे
"Kṣetrajña," the knower of the field (a Bhagavad Gītā term for the witnessing self, Gītā XIII.1-2), opens directly on kṣa — the single most doctrinally loaded kṣa-opening in this entire index, since it names the very distinction between the observed body and the observing awareness that Part IX below returns to repeatedly.
